# Dinosaur

For other uses, see [Dinosaur (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_\(disambiguation\)).

Not to be confused with [Dinosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaurus).

montage of four birds

[Birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird) are avian dinosaurs, and [phylogenetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phylogenetics) [taxonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxonomy_\(biology\)) includes over 11,000 extant species in the group Dinosauria.

**Dinosaurs** are a diverse group of warmblooded [reptiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reptile)[^cite_note-cold-blooded-7] of the [clade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clade) **Dinosauria**. They existed through most of the [Mesozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesozoic) [era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erathem), first appearing early in the [Triassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triassic) [period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geological_period). They became the dominant terrestrial [vertebrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebrate) after the [Triassic–Jurassic extinction event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triassic%E2%80%93Jurassic_extinction_event) 201.3 [mya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Million_years_ago) and their dominance continued throughout the [Jurassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic) and [Cretaceous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretaceous) periods. The [fossil](10958.md) record shows that [birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird) are [feathered dinosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feathered_dinosaurs), [having evolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution_of_birds) from earlier [theropods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theropoda) during the [Late Jurassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Jurassic) [epoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epoch), and are the only dinosaur lineage known to have survived the [Cretaceous–Paleogene extinction event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretaceous%E2%80%93Paleogene_extinction_event) approximately 66 mya. Dinosaurs can therefore be divided into **avian dinosaurs**—birds—and the extinct **non-avian dinosaurs**, which are all dinosaurs other than birds.

Dinosaurs are varied from [taxonomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxonomy_\(biology\)), [morphological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphology_\(biology\)) and [ecological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological) standpoints. Birds, at over 11,000 living [species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Species), are among the most diverse groups of vertebrates. Using fossil evidence, [paleontologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleontologists) have identified over 900 distinct [genera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genera) and more than 1,000 different species of non-avian dinosaurs. Dinosaurs are represented on every continent by both [extant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neontology) species (birds) and fossil remains. Through most of the 20th century, before birds were recognized as dinosaurs, most of the scientific community presumed dinosaurs to have been sluggish and [cold-blooded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poikilotherm). Most [research conducted since the 1970s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_renaissance), however, has indicated that dinosaurs were active animals with elevated [metabolisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metabolism) and numerous adaptations for social interaction. Evidence shows that all dinosaurs had erect legs similar to that of birds and many mammals, as opposed to the sprawling leg stance of cold-blooded tetrapods. Some were [herbivorous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbivorous), others [carnivorous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnivorous). Evidence suggests that all dinosaurs were [egg-laying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egg-laying), and that [nest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nest)-building was a trait shared by many dinosaurs, both avian and non-avian.

While dinosaurs were ancestrally [bipedal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipedal), many [extinct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extinct) groups included [quadrupedal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrupedal) species, and some were able to shift between these stances. Elaborate display structures such as horns or crests are common to all dinosaur groups, and some extinct groups developed [skeletal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skeletal) modifications such as [bony armor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armour_\(anatomy\)) and [spines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spine_\(zoology\)). While the dinosaurs' modern-day surviving avian lineage (birds) are generally small due to the constraints of flight, many non-avian dinosaurs were large-bodied—the largest [sauropod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauropoda) dinosaurs are estimated to have reached lengths of 39.7 meters (130 feet) and heights of 18 m (59 ft) and were the largest land animals of all time.

The first dinosaur fossils were recognized in the early 19th century, with the name "dinosaur" (meaning "terrible lizard") being coined by Sir [Richard Owen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Owen) in 1842 to refer to these "great fossil lizards". Since then, mounted fossil dinosaur skeletons have been major attractions at museums worldwide, and dinosaurs have become [an enduring part of popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_depictions_of_dinosaurs). The large sizes of some dinosaurs, as well as their seemingly monstrous and fantastic nature, have ensured their regular appearance in best-selling books and films, such as the *[Jurassic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic_Park_\(franchise\))* franchise. Persistent public enthusiasm for the animals has resulted in significant funding for dinosaur science, and new discoveries are regularly covered by the media.

## Etymology

in 1842 the English paleontologist [Sir Richard Owen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_Richard_Owen) coined the term "dinosaur", using it to refer to the "distinct tribe or sub-order of Saurian Reptiles" that were then being recognized in England and around the world.[^cite_note-\:2-8][^cite_note-\:3-9][^cite_note-\:4-10][^cite_note-Owen1841-11][^cite_note-12] The term is derived from [Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language)&#x20;*&#x20;*[δεινός](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CE%B4%CE%B5%CE%B9%CE%BD%CF%8C%CF%82)*&#x20;(deinos)* 'terrible, potent or fearfully great' and&#x20;*&#x20;*[σαῦρος](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CF%83%CE%B1%E1%BF%A6%CF%81%CE%BF%CF%82)*&#x20;(sauros)* 'lizard or reptile'.[^cite_note-Owen1841-11][^cite_note-LSJ-13] Though the taxonomic name has often been interpreted as a reference to dinosaurs' teeth, claws, and other fearsome characteristics, Owen intended it also to evoke their size and majesty.[^cite_note-FBS97-14]

## Definition

Under [phylogenetic nomenclature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phylogenetic_nomenclature), dinosaurs are usually defined as the group consisting of the [most recent common ancestor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Most_recent_common_ancestor) (MRCA) of *[Triceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triceratops)* and [modern birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird#Diversification_of_modern_birds) (Neornithes), and all its descendants.[^cite_note-MJB04dino-15] It has also been suggested that Dinosauria be defined with respect to the MRCA of *[Megalosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalosaurus)* and *[Iguanodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iguanodon)*, because these were two of the three genera cited by Richard Owen when he recognized the Dinosauria.[^cite_note-olshevsky2000-16] Both definitions cover the same known genera: Dinosauria = [Ornithischia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithischia) + [Saurischia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saurischia). This includes major groups such as [ankylosaurians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ankylosauria) (armored herbivorous quadrupeds), [stegosaurians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stegosauria) (plated herbivorous quadrupeds), [ceratopsians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratopsia) (bipedal or quadrupedal herbivores with [neck frills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neck_frill)), [pachycephalosaurians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pachycephalosauria) (bipedal herbivores with thick skulls), [ornithopods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithopoda) (bipedal or quadrupedal herbivores including "[duck-bills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrosauridae)"), [theropods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theropoda) (mostly bipedal carnivores and birds), and [sauropodomorphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauropodomorpha) (mostly large herbivorous quadrupeds with long necks and tails).[^cite_note-Letal05-17]

Birds are the sole surviving dinosaurs. In traditional [taxonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxonomy_\(biology\)), birds were considered a separate [class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_\(biology\)) that had evolved from dinosaurs. However, most contemporary paleontologists reject the traditional style of classification based on anatomical similarity, in favor of [phylogenetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phylogenetics) taxonomy based on deduced ancestry, in which each group is defined as all descendants of a given founding genus.[^cite_note-18] Birds belong to the dinosaur subgroup [Maniraptora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maniraptora), which are [coelurosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coelurosauria), which are theropods, which are saurischians.[^cite_note-KP04-19]

Research by Matthew G. Baron, [David B. Norman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_B._Norman), and Paul M. Barrett in 2017 suggested a radical revision of dinosaurian systematics. Phylogenetic analysis by Baron et al. recovered the Ornithischia as being closer to the Theropoda than the Sauropodomorpha, as opposed to the traditional union of theropods with sauropodomorphs. This would cause sauropods and kin to fall outside traditional dinosaurs, so they re-defined Dinosauria as the last common ancestor of *Triceratops horridus*, *[Passer domesticus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_sparrow)* and *[Diplodocus carnegii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplodocus)*, and all of its descendants, to ensure that sauropods and kin remain included as dinosaurs. They also resurrected the clade [Ornithoscelida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithoscelida) to refer to the group containing Ornithischia and Theropoda.[^cite_note-NYT-20170322-20][^cite_note-NAT-20170322-21]

### General description



*[Triceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triceratops)* skeleton, [Natural History Museum of Los Angeles County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_History_Museum_of_Los_Angeles_County)

Using one of the above definitions, dinosaurs can be generally described as [archosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archosaur) with [hind limbs held erect beneath the body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrestrial_locomotion#Posture).[^cite_note-DFG97-22] Other prehistoric animals, including [pterosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pterosaur), [mosasaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosasaur), [ichthyosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ichthyosauria), [plesiosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plesiosauria), and *[Dimetrodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimetrodon)*, while often popularly conceived of as dinosaurs, are not taxonomically classified as dinosaurs. Pterosaurs are distantly related to dinosaurs, being members of the clade [Ornithodira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avemetatarsalia). The other groups mentioned are, like dinosaurs and pterosaurs, members of [Sauropsida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauropsida) (the reptile and bird clade), except *Dimetrodon* (which is a [synapsid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synapsid)). None of them had the erect hind limb posture characteristic of true dinosaurs.[^cite_note-DL90-23]

Dinosaurs were the dominant terrestrial vertebrates of the [Mesozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesozoic) [Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era), especially the Jurassic and Cretaceous periods. Other groups of animals were restricted in size and niches; [mammals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal), for example, rarely exceeded the size of a domestic cat and were generally rodent-sized carnivores of small prey.[^cite_note-MM97-24] Dinosaurs have always been recognized as an extremely varied group: over 900 non-avian dinosaur genera have been confidently identified (2018) with 1,124 species (2016). Estimates put the total number of dinosaur genera preserved in the fossil record at 1850, nearly 75% still undiscovered,[^cite_note-Genera900-25][^cite_note-Howmany-26][^cite_note-Wang\&Dodson-27] and the number that ever existed (in or out of the fossil record) at 3,400.[^cite_note-russell1995-28] A 2016 estimate put the number of dinosaur species living in the Mesozoic at 1,543–2,468,[^cite_note-Howmany-26][^cite_note-29] compared to the number of modern-day birds (avian dinosaurs) at 10,806 species.[^cite_note-30]

Extinct dinosaurs, as well as modern birds, include genera that are herbivorous and others carnivorous, including seed-eaters, fish-eaters, insectivores, and omnivores. While dinosaurs were ancestrally bipedal (as are all modern birds), some evolved into quadrupeds, and others, such as *[Anchisaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchisaurus)* and *Iguanodon*, could walk as easily on two or four legs. Cranial modifications like horns and crests are common dinosaurian traits, and some extinct species had bony armor. Although the best-known genera are remarkable for their large size, many Mesozoic dinosaurs were human-sized or smaller, and modern birds are generally small in size. Dinosaurs today inhabit every continent, and fossils show that they had achieved global distribution by the [Early Jurassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Jurassic) epoch at latest.[^cite_note-macleod1997-31] Modern birds inhabit most available habitats, from terrestrial to marine, and there is evidence that some non-avian dinosaurs (such as *[Microraptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microraptor)*) could fly or at least glide, and others, such as [spinosaurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinosauridae), had [semiaquatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiaquatic) habits.[^cite_note-theropods-32]

### Distinguishing anatomical features

While recent discoveries have made it more difficult to present a universally agreed-upon list of their distinguishing features, nearly all dinosaurs discovered so far share certain modifications to the ancestral archosaurian skeleton, or are clearly descendants of older dinosaurs showing these modifications. Although some later groups of dinosaurs featured further modified versions of these traits, they are considered typical for Dinosauria; the earliest dinosaurs had them and passed them on to their descendants. Such modifications, originating in the most recent common ancestor of a certain taxonomic group, are called the [synapomorphies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synapomorphy_and_apomorphy) of such a group.[^cite_note-B012-33]



Labeled diagram of a typical archosaur skull, the skull of *[Dromaeosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dromaeosaurus)*

A detailed assessment of archosaur interrelations by [Sterling Nesbitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterling_Nesbitt)[^cite_note-nesbitt2011-34] confirmed or found the following twelve unambiguous synapomorphies, some previously known:

- In the [skull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skull), a supratemporal fossa (excavation) is present in front of the [supratemporal fenestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skull#Structure), the main opening in the rear skull roof

- [Epipophyses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epipophyses), obliquely backward-pointing processes on the rear top corners of the anterior (front) neck [vertebrae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebra) behind the [atlas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlas_\(anatomy\)) and [axis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_\(anatomy\)), the first two neck vertebrae

- Apex of a deltopectoral crest (a projection on which the [deltopectoral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clavipectoral_triangle) muscles attach) located at or more than 30% down the length of the [humerus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humerus) (upper arm bone)

- [Radius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radius_\(bone\)), a lower arm bone, shorter than 80% of humerus length

- [Fourth trochanter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_trochanter) (projection where the [caudofemoralis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caudofemoralis) muscle attaches on the inner rear shaft) on the [femur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Femur) (thigh bone) is a sharp flange

- Fourth trochanter asymmetrical, with distal, lower, margin forming a steeper angle to the shaft

- On the [astragalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talus_bone) and [calcaneum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcaneus), upper ankle bones, the proximal articular facet, the top connecting surface, for the [fibula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fibula) occupies less than 30% of the transverse width of the element

- Exoccipitals (bones at the back of the skull) do not meet along the midline on the floor of the endocranial cavity, the inner space of the braincase

- In the pelvis, the proximal articular surfaces of the [ischium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ischium) with the [ilium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilium_\(bone\)) and the [pubis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pubis_\(bone\)) are separated by a large concave surface (on the upper side of the ischium a part of the open hip joint is located between the contacts with the pubic bone and the ilium)

- [Cnemial crest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cnemial_crest) on the [tibia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibia) (protruding part of the top surface of the shinbone) arcs anterolaterally (curves to the front and the outer side)

- Distinct proximodistally oriented (vertical) ridge present on the posterior face of the distal end of the tibia (the rear surface of the lower end of the shinbone)

- Concave articular surface for the fibula of the calcaneum (the top surface of the calcaneum, where it touches the fibula, has a hollow profile)

Nesbitt found a number of further potential synapomorphies and discounted a number of synapomorphies previously suggested. Some of these are also present in [silesaurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silesauridae), which Nesbitt recovered as a sister group to Dinosauria, including a large anterior trochanter, metatarsals II and IV of subequal length, reduced contact between ischium and pubis, the presence of a cnemial crest on the tibia and of an ascending process on the astragalus, and many others.[^cite_note-MJB04dino-15]



Hip joints and hindlimb postures of: (left to right) typical [reptiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reptile) (sprawling), dinosaurs and [mammals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal) (erect), and [rauisuchians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rauisuchia) (pillar-erect)

A variety of other skeletal features are shared by dinosaurs. However, because they either are common to other groups of archosaurs or were not present in all early dinosaurs, these features are not synapomorphies. For example, as [diapsids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diapsid), dinosaurs ancestrally had two pairs of [Infratemporal fenestrae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infratemporal_fenestra) (openings in the skull behind the eyes), and as members of the diapsid group Archosauria, had additional openings in the [snout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antorbital_fenestra) and lower jaw.[^cite_note-TRHJ00-35] Additionally, several characteristics once thought to be synapomorphies are now known to have appeared before dinosaurs, or were absent in the earliest dinosaurs and independently evolved by different dinosaur groups. These include an elongated [scapula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scapula), or shoulder blade; a [sacrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacrum) composed of three or more fused vertebrae (three are found in some other archosaurs, but only two are found in *[Herrerasaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herrerasaurus)*);[^cite_note-MJB04dino-15] and a perforate [acetabulum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acetabulum), or hip socket, with a hole at the center of its inside surface (closed in *[Saturnalia tupiniquim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturnalia_tupiniquim)*, for example).[^cite_note-UC_Berkeley_Journal_of_Earth_Sciences-36][^cite_note-LARB99-37] Another difficulty of determining distinctly dinosaurian features is that early dinosaurs and other archosaurs from the [Late Triassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Triassic) epoch are often poorly known and were similar in many ways; these animals have sometimes been misidentified in the literature.[^cite_note-NIP07-38]

Dinosaurs stand with their hind limbs erect in a manner similar to [most modern mammals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution_of_mammals#Erect_limbs), but distinct from most other reptiles, whose limbs sprawl out to either side.[^cite_note-Holland1909-39] This posture is due to the development of a laterally facing recess in the pelvis (usually an open socket) and a corresponding inwardly facing distinct head on the femur.[^cite_note-MJB00-40] Their erect posture enabled early dinosaurs to breathe easily while moving, which likely permitted stamina and activity levels that [surpassed those of "sprawling" reptiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrier's_constraint).[^cite_note-RC05-41] Erect limbs probably also helped support the [evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution) of large size by reducing bending stresses on limbs.[^cite_note-TKMB07-42] Some non-dinosaurian archosaurs, including [rauisuchians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rauisuchia), also had erect limbs but achieved this by a "pillar-erect" configuration of the hip joint, where instead of having a projection from the femur insert on a socket on the hip, the [upper pelvic bone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilium_\(bone\)) was rotated to form an overhanging shelf.[^cite_note-TKMB07-42]

## History of study

Further information: [History of paleontology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_paleontology)

### Pre-scientific history

Dinosaur fossils have been known for millennia, although their true nature was not recognized. Chinese authors have long recognised remains of prehistoric animals as "[dragon bones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon_bones)". *[Huayang Guo Zhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronicles_of_Huayang)* (華陽國志), a [gazetteer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazetteer) compiled by [Chang Qu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang_Qu) (常璩) during the [Western Jin Dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jin_dynasty_\(266%E2%80%93420\)) (265–316), reported the discovery of dragon bones at Wucheng in [Sichuan](65185.md) Province.[^cite_note-dong1992-43] These "dragon bones" were and still are often used as ingredients for remedies in [Traditional Chinese Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_Medicine).[^cite_note-BBCdinobonemed-44] While these "dragon bones" have often popularly been claimed to be dinosaur bones, all known historical examples of vertebrate "dragon bones" from China are actually from [Cenozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cenozoic) mammals.[^cite_note-45] However, a village in [Henan](166408.md), [central China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_China) was reported in 2007 to have spent the previous several decades excavating and using real dinosaur bones as "dragon bones" for TCM purposes.[^cite_note-BBCdinobonemed-44] In [Europe](9239.md), dinosaur fossils were generally thought to be the remains of [giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giant) and other [biblical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) creatures.[^cite_note-benton2000-46]

### Early dinosaur research



[William Buckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Buckland)

Scholarly descriptions of what would now be recognized as dinosaur bones first appeared in the late 17th century in England. Part of a bone, now known to have been the femur of a *[Megalosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalosaurus)*,[^cite_note-WAS97-47] was recovered from a limestone quarry at [Cornwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwell,_Oxfordshire) near [Chipping Norton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chipping_Norton), Oxfordshire, in 1676. The fragment was sent to [Robert Plot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Plot), Professor of Chemistry at the [University of Oxford](31797.md) and first curator of the [Ashmolean Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashmolean_Museum), who published a description in his *The Natural History of Oxford-shire* (1677).[^cite_note-48] He correctly identified the bone as the lower extremity of the femur of a large animal, and recognized that it was too large to belong to any known species. He therefore concluded it to be the femur of a huge human, perhaps a [Titan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titan_\(mythology\)) or another type of giant featured in legends.[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50] [Edward Lhuyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Lhuyd), a friend of [Sir Isaac Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton), published *Lithophylacii Britannici ichnographia* (1699), the first scientific treatment of what would now be recognized as a dinosaur. In it he described and named a sauropod [tooth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tooth), "[Rutellum impicatum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutellum)",[^cite_note-L99-51][^cite_note-DS02-52] that had been found in Caswell, near [Witney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witney), Oxfordshire.[^cite_note-G45-53]



Sir [Richard Owen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Owen)'s coining of the word *dinosaur*, in the 1842 revised version of his talk at an 1841 meeting of the [British Association for the Advancement of Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_association_for_the_advancement_of_science).

Between 1815 and 1824, the Rev [William Buckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Buckland), the first Reader of Geology at the University of Oxford, collected more fossilized bones of *Megalosaurus* and became the first person to describe a non-avian dinosaur in a [scientific journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_journal).[^cite_note-WAS97-47][^cite_note-buckland1824-54] The second non-avian dinosaur genus to be identified, *[Iguanodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iguanodon)*, was purportedly discovered in 1822 by [Mary Ann Mantell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Ann_Mantell), the wife of English geologist [Gideon Mantell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gideon_Mantell), though this is disputed and some historians say Gideon had acquired remains years earlier. Gideon Mantell recognized similarities between his fossils and the bones of modern [iguanas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iguana) and published his findings in 1825.[^cite_note-GM25-55][^cite_note-HDS97-56]

The study of these "great fossil lizards" soon became of great interest to European and American scientists. Owen recognized that the remains that had been found so far, *Iguanodon*, *Megalosaurus* and *[Hylaeosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hylaeosaurus)*, shared distinctive features, and so decided to present them as a distinct taxonomic group. As clarified by British geologist and historian Hugh Torrens, Owen had given a presentation about fossil reptiles to the British Association for the Advancement of Science in 1841, but reports of the time show that Owen did not mention the word "dinosaur", nor recognize dinosaurs as a distinct group of reptiles in his address. He introduced the Dinosauria only in the revised text version of his talk published in April 1842.[^cite_note-\:2-8][^cite_note-\:3-9] With the backing of [Prince Albert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Albert_of_Saxe-Coburg_and_Gotha), the husband of [Queen Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Victoria), Owen established the [Natural History Museum, London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_History_Museum,_London), to display the national collection of dinosaur fossils and other biological and geological exhibits.[^cite_note-owen94-57]

### Discoveries in North America



[Edward Drinker Cope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Drinker_Cope)



[Othniel Charles Marsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Othniel_Charles_Marsh)

In 1858, [William Parker Foulke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Parker_Foulke) discovered the first known American dinosaur in [marl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marl) pits in the small town of [Haddonfield, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haddonfield,_New_Jersey). (Although fossils had been found before, their nature had not been correctly discerned.) The creature was named *[Hadrosaurus foulkii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrosaurus)*. It was an extremely important find: *Hadrosaurus* was one of the first nearly complete dinosaur skeletons found ([the first](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iguanodon#Gideon_Mantell,_Sir_Richard_Owen,_and_the_discovery_of_dinosaurs) was in 1834, in [Maidstone, England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maidstone)), and it was clearly a bipedal creature. This was a revolutionary discovery as, until that point, most scientists had assumed dinosaurs walked on four feet, like other lizards. Foulke's discoveries sparked a wave of interests in dinosaurs in the United States, known as Dinosaur Mania.[^cite_note-weishampel06-58]

Dinosaur mania was exemplified by the fierce rivalry between [Edward Drinker Cope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Drinker_Cope) and [Othniel Charles Marsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Othniel_Charles_Marsh), both of whom raced to be the first to find new dinosaurs in what came to be known as the [Bone Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bone_Wars). This fight between the two scientists lasted for over 30 years, ending in 1897 when Cope died after spending his entire fortune on the dinosaur hunt. Many valuable dinosaur specimens were damaged or destroyed due to the pair's rough methods: for example, their diggers often used [dynamite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynamite) to unearth bones. Modern paleontologists would find such methods crude and unacceptable, since blasting easily destroys fossil and stratigraphic evidence. Despite their unrefined methods, the contributions of Cope and Marsh to paleontology were vast: Marsh unearthed 86 new species of dinosaur and Cope discovered 56, a total of 142 new species. Cope's collection is now at the [American Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Museum_of_Natural_History) in New York City, while Marsh's is at the [Peabody Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peabody_Museum_of_Natural_History) at [Yale University](34273.md).[^cite_note-Holmes-59]

### "Dinosaur renaissance" and beyond

Main article: [Dinosaur renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_renaissance)

[World War II](32927.md) caused a pause in palaeontological research; after the war, research attention was also diverted increasingly to fossil mammals rather than dinosaurs, which were seen as sluggish and cold-blooded.[^cite_note-taylor2010-60][^cite_note-Naish-61] At the end of the 1960s, however, the field of dinosaur research experienced a surge in activity that remains ongoing.[^cite_note-arbour2018-62] Several seminal studies led to this activity. First, [John Ostrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ostrom) discovered the bird-like [dromaeosaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dromaeosauridae) theropod *[Deinonychus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deinonychus)* and described it in 1969. Its anatomy indicated that it was an active predator that was likely warm-blooded, in marked contrast to the then-prevailing image of dinosaurs.[^cite_note-taylor2010-60] Concurrently, [Robert T. Bakker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_T._Bakker) published a series of studies that likewise argued for active lifestyles in dinosaurs based on anatomical and ecological evidence (see [§ Physiology](#Physiology)),[^cite_note-bakker1968-63][^cite_note-bakker1972-64] which were subsequently summarized in his 1986 book *[The Dinosaur Heresies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dinosaur_Heresies)*.[^cite_note-bakker86-65]



Paleontologist [Robert T. Bakker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_T._Bakker) with a mounted skeleton of a [tyrannosaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosauridae) (*[Gorgosaurus libratus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorgosaurus)*)

New revelations were supported by an increase in dinosaur discoveries. Major new dinosaur discoveries have been made by paleontologists working in previously unexplored regions, including India, South America, Madagascar, Antarctica, and most significantly China. Across theropods, sauropodomorphs, and ornithischians, the number of named genera began to increase drastically in the 1990s,[^cite_note-Genera900-25] with up to 30 new species of dinosaurs named each year by 2008.[^cite_note-benton2008-66] At least sauropodomorphs experienced a further increase in the number of named species in the 2010s, with an average of 9.3 new species having been named each year between 2009 and 2020. As a consequence, more sauropodomorphs were named between 1990 and 2020 than in all previous years combined.[^cite_note-cashmore2020-67] These new localities also led to improvements in overall specimen quality, with new species being increasingly named not on scrappy fossils but on more complete skeletons, sometimes from multiple individuals. Better specimens also led to new species being invalidated less frequently.[^cite_note-benton2008-66] Asian localities have produced the most complete theropod specimens,[^cite_note-cashmore2019-68] while North American localities have produced the most complete sauropodomorph specimens.[^cite_note-cashmore2020-67]

Prior to the dinosaur renaissance, dinosaurs were mostly classified using the traditional rank-based system of [Linnaean taxonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linnaean_taxonomy). The renaissance was also accompanied by the increasingly widespread application of [cladistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cladistics), a more objective method of classification based on ancestry and shared traits, which has proved tremendously useful in the study of dinosaur systematics and evolution. Cladistic analysis, among other techniques, helps to compensate for an often incomplete and fragmentary fossil record.[^cite_note-holtz1997-69][^cite_note-NYT-20161208-70] Reference books summarizing the state of dinosaur research, such as [David B. Weishampel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_B._Weishampel) and colleagues' *[The Dinosauria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dinosauria)*, made knowledge more accessible[^cite_note-lockley2000-71] and spurred further interest in dinosaur research. The release of the first and second editions of *The Dinosauria* in 1990 and 2004, and of a review paper by [Paul Sereno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Sereno) in 1998, were accompanied by increases in the number of published [phylogenetic trees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phylogenetic_tree) for dinosaurs.[^cite_note-lloyd2008-72]

### Soft tissue and molecular preservation



An *[Edmontosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmontosaurus)* specimen's skin impressions found in 1999

Dinosaur fossils are not limited to bones, but also include imprints or mineralized remains of skin coverings, organs, and other tissues. Of these, skin coverings based on [keratin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keratin) proteins are most easily preserved because of their [cross-linked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-link), [hydrophobic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrophobe) molecular structure.[^cite_note-schweitzer2011-73] Fossils of keratin-based skin coverings or bony skin coverings are known from most major groups of dinosaurs. Dinosaur fossils with scaly skin impressions have been found since the 19th century. [Samuel Beckles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Beckles) discovered a sauropod forelimb with preserved skin in 1852 that was incorrectly attributed to a crocodile; it was correctly attributed by Marsh in 1888 and subject to further study by [Reginald Hooley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reginald_Hooley) in 1917.[^cite_note-hooley1917-74] Among ornithischians, in 1884 Jacob Wortman found skin impressions on the first known specimen of *[Edmontosaurus annectens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmontosaurus_annectens)*, which were largely destroyed during the specimen's excavation.[^cite_note-osborn1912-75] Owen and Hooley subsequently described skin impressions of *[Hypsilophodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypsilophodon)* and *Iguanodon* in 1885 and 1917.[^cite_note-hooley1917-74] Since then, scale impressions have been most frequently found among hadrosaurids, where the impressions are known from nearly the entire body across multiple specimens.[^cite_note-bell2014-76]



Color restoration of *[Sinosauropteryx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinosauropteryx)*



Color restoration of *[Psittacosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psittacosaurus)*

Starting from the 1990s, major discoveries of exceptionally preserved fossils in deposits known as conservation [Lagerstätten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagerst%C3%A4tte) contributed to research on dinosaur soft tissues.[^cite_note-eliason2017-77][^cite_note-benton1998-78] Chiefly among these were the rocks that produced the [Jehol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehol_Biota) (Early Cretaceous) and [Yanliao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanliao_Biota) (Mid-to-Late Jurassic) [biotas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biota_\(ecology\)) of northeastern China, from which hundreds of dinosaur specimens bearing impressions of feather-like structures (both closely related to birds and otherwise, see [§ Origin of birds](#Origin_of_birds)) have been described by [Xing Xu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xu_Xing_\(paleontologist\)) and colleagues.[^cite_note-zhou2017-79][^cite_note-norell2005-80] In living reptiles and mammals, pigment-storing cellular structures known as [melanosomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanosome) are partially responsible for producing colouration.[^cite_note-roy2020-81][^cite_note-vinther2020-82] Both chemical traces of [melanin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanin) and characteristically shaped melanosomes have been reported from feathers and scales of Jehol and Yanliao dinosaurs, including both theropods and ornithischians.[^cite_note-zhang2010-83] This has enabled multiple full-body reconstructions of [dinosaur colouration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_colouration), such as for *[Sinosauropteryx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinosauropteryx)*[^cite_note-smithwick2017-84] and *[Psittacosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psittacosaurus)*[^cite_note-vinther2016-85] by Jakob Vinther and colleagues, and similar techniques have also been extended to dinosaur fossils from other localities.[^cite_note-roy2020-81] (However, some researchers have also suggested that fossilized melanosomes represent bacterial remains.[^cite_note-lindgren2015-86][^cite_note-schweitzer2015-87]) Stomach contents in some Jehol and Yanliao dinosaurs closely related to birds have also provided indirect indications of diet and digestive system anatomy (e.g., [crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crop_\(anatomy\))).[^cite_note-zhou2014-88][^cite_note-oconnor2019-89] More concrete evidence of internal anatomy has been reported in *[Scipionyx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scipionyx)* from the [Pietraroja Plattenkalk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pietraroja_Plattenkalk) of Italy. It preserves portions of the intestines, colon, liver, muscles, and windpipe.[^cite_note-softtissue-90]



*[Scipionyx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scipionyx)* fossil with intestines, [Natural History Museum of Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Civico_di_Storia_Naturale_di_Milano)

Concurrently, a line of work led by [Mary Higby Schweitzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Higby_Schweitzer), [Jack Horner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Horner_\(paleontologist\)), and colleagues reported various occurrences of preserved soft tissues and proteins within dinosaur bone fossils. Various mineralized structures that likely represented [red blood cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_blood_cell) and [collagen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collagen) fibres had been found by Schweitzer and others in [tyrannosaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyrannosauridae) bones as early as 1991.[^cite_note-morell1993-91][^cite_note-pawlicki1996-92][^cite_note-pawlicki1998-93] However, in 2005, Schweitzer and colleagues reported that a femur of *[Tyrannosaurus](30467.md)* preserved soft, flexible tissue within, including [blood vessels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_vessel), [bone matrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osteon), and connective tissue (bone fibers) that had retained their microscopic structure.[^cite_note-Schweitzer2005-94] This discovery suggested that original soft tissues could be preserved over geological time,[^cite_note-schweitzer2011-73] with multiple mechanisms having been proposed.[^cite_note-anderson2023-95] Later, in 2009, Schweitzer and colleagues reported that a *[Brachylophosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brachylophosaurus)* femur preserved similar microstructures, and [immunohistochemical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunohistochemistry) techniques (based on [antibody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antibody) binding) demonstrated the presence of proteins such as collagen, [elastin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elastin), and [laminin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laminin).[^cite_note-schweitzer2009-96] Both specimens yielded collagen protein sequences that were viable for [molecular phylogenetic analyses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_phylogenetics), which grouped them with birds as would be expected.[^cite_note-schweitzer2009-96][^cite_note-organ2008-97] The extraction of fragmentary DNA has also been reported for both of these fossils,[^cite_note-schweitzer2013-98] along with a specimen of *[Hypacrosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypacrosaurus)*.[^cite_note-bailleul2020-99] In 2015, Sergio Bertazzo and colleagues reported the preservation of collagen fibres and red blood cells in eight Cretaceous dinosaur specimens that did not show any signs of exceptional preservation, indicating that soft tissue may be preserved more commonly than previously thought.[^cite_note-bertazzo2015-100] Suggestions that these structures represent bacterial [biofilms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biofilm)[^cite_note-kaye2008-101] have been rejected,[^cite_note-peterson2010-102] but cross-contamination remains a possibility that is difficult to detect.[^cite_note-buckley2017-103]

## Evolutionary history

### Origins and early evolution

Full skeleton of an early carnivorous dinosaur, displayed in a glass case in a museum

The early dinosaurs *[Herrerasaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herrerasaurus)* (large), *[Eoraptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eoraptor)* (small) and a *[Plateosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plateosaurus)* skull, from the [Triassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triassic)

Dinosaurs diverged from their archosaur ancestors during the Middle to Late Triassic epochs, roughly 20 million years after the devastating [Permian–Triassic extinction event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permian%E2%80%93Triassic_extinction_event) wiped out an estimated 96% of all marine species and 70% of terrestrial vertebrate species approximately 252 million years ago.[^cite_note-KPA-104][^cite_note-TannerLucas-105] The oldest dinosaur fossils known from substantial remains date to the [Carnian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnian) epoch of the Triassic period and have been found primarily in the [Ischigualasto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ischigualasto_Formation) and [Santa Maria Formations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Maria_Formation) of Argentina and Brazil, and the [Pebbly Arkose Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pebbly_Arkose_Formation) of Zimbabwe.[^cite_note-griffin2022-106]

The Ischigualasto Formation ([radiometrically dated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiometric_dating) at 231–230 million years old[^cite_note-desojo2020-107]) has produced the early saurischian *[Eoraptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eoraptor)*, originally considered a member of the [Herrerasauridae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herrerasauridae)[^cite_note-OARM2010-108] but now considered to be an early sauropodomorph, along with the herrerasaurids *Herrerasaurus* and *[Sanjuansaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanjuansaurus)*, and the sauropodomorphs *[Chromogisaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromogisaurus)*, *[Eodromaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eodromaeus)*, and *[Panphagia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panphagia)*.[^cite_note-novas2021-109] *Eoraptor*'s likely resemblance to the [common ancestor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_descent) of all dinosaurs suggests that the first dinosaurs would have been small, bipedal [predators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predation).[^cite_note-Daemonosaurus-110][^cite_note-Sereno1999-111][^cite_note-SFRM93-112] The Santa Maria Formation (radiometrically dated to be older, at 233.23 million years old[^cite_note-langer18-113]) has produced the herrerasaurids *[Gnathovorax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gnathovorax)* and *[Staurikosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staurikosaurus)*, along with the sauropodomorphs *[Bagualosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagualosaurus)*, *[Buriolestes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buriolestes)*, *[Guaibasaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guaibasaurus)*, *[Macrocollum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macrocollum)*, *[Nhandumirim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nhandumirim)*, *[Pampadromaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pampadromaeus)*, *Saturnalia*, and *[Unaysaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unaysaurus)*.[^cite_note-novas2021-109] The Pebbly Arkose Formation, which is of uncertain age but was likely comparable to the other two, has produced the sauropodomorph *[Mbiresaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mbiresaurus)*, along with an unnamed herrerasaurid.[^cite_note-griffin2022-106]

Less well-preserved remains of the sauropodomorphs *[Jaklapallisaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaklapallisaurus)* and *[Nambalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nambalia)*, along with the early saurischian *[Alwalkeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alwalkeria)*, are known from the [Upper Maleri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Maleri_Formation) and [Lower Maleri Formations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Maleri_Formation) of India.[^cite_note-novas2011-114] The Carnian-aged [Chañares Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cha%C3%B1ares_Formation) of Argentina preserves primitive, dinosaur-like ornithodirans such as *[Lagosuchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagosuchus)* and *[Lagerpeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagerpeton)* in [Argentina](18951905.md), making it another important site for understanding dinosaur evolution. These ornithodirans support the model of early dinosaurs as small, bipedal predators.[^cite_note-novas2021-109][^cite_note-marsicano2015-115] Dinosaurs may have appeared as early as the [Anisian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anisian) epoch of the Triassic, approximately 243 million years ago, which is the age of *[Nyasasaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nyasasaurus)* from the [Manda Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manda_Formation) of Tanzania. However, its known fossils are too fragmentary to identify it as a dinosaur or only a close relative.[^cite_note-nyasasaurus-116] The referral of the Manda Formation to the Anisian is also uncertain. Regardless, dinosaurs existed alongside non-dinosaurian ornithodirans for a period of time, with estimates ranging from 5–10 million years[^cite_note-marsicano2015-115] to 21 million years.[^cite_note-langer18-113]

When dinosaurs appeared, they were not the dominant terrestrial animals. The terrestrial habitats were occupied by various types of [archosauromorphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archosauromorpha) and [therapsids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Therapsid), like [cynodonts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynodont) and [rhynchosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhynchosaur). Their main competitors were the [pseudosuchians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudosuchians), such as [aetosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aetosaur), [ornithosuchids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithosuchidae) and rauisuchians, which were more successful than the dinosaurs.[^cite_note-117] Most of these other animals became extinct in the Triassic, in one of two events. First, at about 215 million years ago, a variety of [basal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basal_\(phylogenetics\)) archosauromorphs, including the [protorosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protorosauria), became extinct. This was followed by the Triassic–Jurassic extinction event (about 201 million years ago), that saw the end of most of the other groups of early archosaurs, like aetosaurs, ornithosuchids, [phytosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phytosaur), and rauisuchians. Rhynchosaurs and [dicynodonts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dicynodont) survived (at least in some areas) at least as late as early –mid [Norian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norian) and late Norian or earliest [Rhaetian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhaetian) [stages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stage_\(stratigraphy\)), respectively,[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119] and the exact date of their [extinction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extinction) is uncertain. These losses left behind a land fauna of [crocodylomorphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocodylomorpha), dinosaurs, mammals, pterosaurians, and [turtles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turtle).[^cite_note-MJB04dino-15] The first few lines of early dinosaurs [diversified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adaptive_radiation) through the Carnian and Norian stages of the Triassic, possibly by occupying the niches of the groups that became extinct.[^cite_note-Letal05-17] Also notably, there was a heightened rate of extinction during the [Carnian pluvial event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnian_pluvial_episode).[^cite_note-120]

### Evolution and paleobiogeography



The supercontinent [Pangaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangaea) in the early [Mesozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesozoic) (around 200 million years ago)

Dinosaur evolution after the Triassic followed changes in vegetation and the location of continents. In the Late Triassic and Early Jurassic, the continents were connected as the single landmass [Pangaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangaea), and there was a worldwide dinosaur fauna mostly composed of [coelophysoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coelophysoidea) carnivores and early sauropodomorph herbivores.[^cite_note-HCL04-121] [Gymnosperm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gymnosperm) plants (particularly [conifers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinophyta)), a potential food source, [radiated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_radiation) in the Late Triassic. Early sauropodomorphs did not have sophisticated mechanisms for processing food in the mouth, and so must have employed other means of breaking down food farther along the digestive tract.[^cite_note-FS04-122] The general homogeneity of dinosaurian faunas continued into the Middle and Late Jurassic, where most localities had predators consisting of [ceratosaurians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratosauria), [megalosauroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalosauroidea), and [allosauroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allosauroidea), and herbivores consisting of stegosaurian ornithischians and large sauropods. Examples of this include the [Morrison Formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morrison_Formation) of [North America](21139.md) and [Tendaguru Beds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tendaguru_Formation) of Tanzania. Dinosaurs in China show some differences, with specialized [metriacanthosaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metriacanthosauridae) theropods and unusual, long-necked sauropods like *[Mamenchisaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamenchisaurus)*.[^cite_note-HCL04-121] Ankylosaurians and ornithopods were also becoming more common, but primitive sauropodomorphs had become extinct. Conifers and [pteridophytes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pteridophyte) were the most common plants. Sauropods, like earlier sauropodomorphs, were not oral processors, but ornithischians were evolving various means of dealing with food in the mouth, including potential [cheek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheek)-like organs to keep food in the mouth, and jaw motions to grind food.[^cite_note-FS04-122] Another notable evolutionary event of the Jurassic was the appearance of true birds, descended from maniraptoran coelurosaurians.[^cite_note-KP04-19]

By the [Early Cretaceous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Cretaceous) and the ongoing breakup of Pangaea, dinosaurs were becoming strongly differentiated by landmass. The earliest part of this time saw the spread of ankylosaurians, [iguanodontians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iguanodontia), and [brachiosaurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brachiosauridae) through Europe, North America, and northern [Africa](5334607.md). These were later supplemented or replaced in Africa by large spinosaurid and [carcharodontosaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carcharodontosauridae) theropods, and [rebbachisaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebbachisauridae) and [titanosaurian](1139347.md) sauropods, also found in [South America](26769.md). In [Asia](689.md), maniraptoran coelurosaurians like dromaeosaurids, [troodontids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troodontidae), and [oviraptorosaurians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oviraptorosauria) became the common theropods, and [ankylosaurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ankylosauridae) and early ceratopsians like *Psittacosaurus* became important herbivores. Meanwhile, [Australia](4689264.md) was home to a fauna of basal ankylosaurians, titanosaurian sauropods and large and small [Elasmarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elasmaria) ornithopods.[^cite_note-HCL04-121] The stegosaurians appear to have gone extinct at some point in the late Early Cretaceous or early [Late Cretaceous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Cretaceous). A major change in the Early Cretaceous, which would be amplified in the Late Cretaceous, was the evolution of [flowering plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flowering_plant). At the same time, several groups of dinosaurian herbivores evolved more sophisticated ways to orally process food. Ceratopsians developed a method of slicing with teeth stacked on each other in batteries, and iguanodontians refined a method of grinding with [dental batteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_tooth#Dental_batteries), taken to its extreme in hadrosaurids.[^cite_note-FS04-122] Some sauropods also evolved tooth batteries, best exemplified by the rebbachisaurid *[Nigersaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigersaurus)*.[^cite_note-serenoetal07-123]

There were three general dinosaur faunas in the Late Cretaceous. In the northern continents of North America and Asia, the major theropods were tyrannosaurids and various types of smaller maniraptoran theropods, with a predominantly ornithischian herbivore assemblage of hadrosaurids, ceratopsians, ankylosaurids, and pachycephalosaurians. In the southern continents that had made up the now-splitting supercontinent [Gondwana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondwana), [abelisaurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abelisauridae) were the common theropods, and titanosaurian sauropods the common herbivores. Finally, in Europe, dromaeosaurids, [rhabdodontid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhabdodontidae) iguanodontians, [nodosaurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nodosauridae) ankylosaurians, and titanosaurian sauropods were prevalent.[^cite_note-HCL04-121] Flowering plants were greatly radiating,[^cite_note-FS04-122] with the first grasses appearing by the end of the Cretaceous.[^cite_note-PSAS05-124] Grinding hadrosaurids and shearing ceratopsians became very diverse across North America and Asia. Theropods were also radiating as herbivores or [omnivores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omnivore), with [therizinosaurians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Therizinosauria) and [ornithomimosaurians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithomimosauria) becoming common.[^cite_note-FS04-122]

The Cretaceous–Paleogene extinction event, which occurred approximately 66 million years ago at the end of the Cretaceous, caused the extinction of all dinosaur groups except for the neornithine birds. Some other diapsid groups, including [crocodilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocodilia), [dyrosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dyrosauridae), [sebecosuchians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebecosuchia), turtles, [lizards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lizard), [snakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snake), [sphenodontians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhynchocephalia), and [choristoderans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choristodera), also survived the event.[^cite_note-AF04-125]

The surviving lineages of neornithine birds, including the ancestors of modern [ratites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratite), [ducks and chickens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fowl), and a variety of [waterbirds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charadriiformes), diversified rapidly at the beginning of the [Paleogene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleogene) period, entering [ecological niches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_niche) left vacant by the extinction of Mesozoic dinosaur groups such as the arboreal [enantiornithines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enantiornithine), aquatic [hesperornithines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesperornithine), and even the larger terrestrial theropods (in the form of *[Gastornis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gastornis)*, [eogruiids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eogruidae), [bathornithids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bathornithids), ratites, [geranoidids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geranoididae), [mihirungs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihirung), and "[terror birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terror_bird)"). It is often stated that mammals out-competed the neornithines for dominance of most terrestrial niches but many of these groups co-existed with rich mammalian faunas for most of the [Cenozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cenozoic) Era.[^cite_note-lindow-126] Terror birds and bathornithids occupied carnivorous guilds alongside predatory mammals,[^cite_note-Cracraft-127][^cite_note-128] and ratites are still fairly successful as midsized herbivores; eogruiids similarly lasted from the [Eocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eocene) to [Pliocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliocene), becoming extinct only very recently after over 20 million years of co-existence with many mammal groups.[^cite_note-129]

## Classification

Main article: [Dinosaur classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_classification)



[Saurischian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saurischia) pelvis structure (left side)



*[Tyrannosaurus](30467.md)* pelvis (showing saurischian structure – left side)



[Ornithischian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithischia) pelvis structure (left side)



*[Edmontosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmontosaurus)* pelvis (showing ornithischian structure – left side)

Dinosaurs belong to a group known as archosaurs, which also includes modern crocodilians. Within the archosaur group, dinosaurs are differentiated most noticeably by their gait. Dinosaur legs extend directly beneath the body, whereas the legs of lizards and crocodilians sprawl out to either side.[^cite_note-B012-33]

Collectively, dinosaurs as a clade are divided into two primary branches, Saurischia and Ornithischia. Saurischia includes those taxa sharing a more recent common ancestor with birds than with Ornithischia, while Ornithischia includes all [taxa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxon) sharing a more recent common ancestor with *Triceratops* than with Saurischia. Anatomically, these two groups can be distinguished most noticeably by their [pelvic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelvis) structure. Early saurischians—"lizard-hipped", from the [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek) *_sauros_* (σαῦρος) meaning "lizard" and *_ischion_* (ἰσχίον) meaning "hip joint"—retained the hip structure of their ancestors, with a pubis bone directed [cranially](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatomical_terms_of_location), or forward.[^cite_note-MJB00-40] This basic form was modified by rotating the pubis backward to varying degrees in several groups (*Herrerasaurus*,[^cite_note-GSP88-130] therizinosauroids,[^cite_note-clarketal2004-131] dromaeosaurids,[^cite_note-MAPM04-132] and birds[^cite_note-KP04-19]). Saurischia includes the theropods (exclusively bipedal and with a wide variety of diets) and sauropodomorphs (long-necked herbivores which include advanced, quadrupedal groups).[^cite_note-theropods-32][^cite_note-sauropodomorphs-133]

By contrast, ornithischians—"bird-hipped", from the Greek *ornis* (ὀρνίς) meaning "bird" and *ischion* (ἰσχίον) meaning "hip joint"—had a pelvis that superficially resembled a bird's pelvis: the pubic bone was oriented caudally (rear-pointing). Unlike birds, the ornithischian pubis also usually had an additional forward-pointing process. Ornithischia includes a variety of species that were primarily herbivores.

Despite the terms "bird hip" (Ornithischia) and "lizard hip" (Saurischia), birds are not part of Ornithischia. Birds instead belong to Saurischia, the "lizard-hipped" dinosaurs—birds evolved from earlier dinosaurs with "lizard hips".[^cite_note-B012-33]

The following cladogram showing the relationships of dinosaur groups is simplified after Michael Benton, 2015:[^cite_note-MJB00-40]

[Theropoda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theropoda)       [Coelophysoidea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coelophysoidea)           [Ceratosauria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceratosauria)     [Tetanurae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetanurae)       [Megalosauroidea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalosauroidea)            [Allosauroidea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allosauroidea)
