## Paleobiology

Knowledge about dinosaurs is derived from a variety of fossil and non-fossil records, including fossilized bones, [feces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feces), [trackways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_trackway), [gastroliths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gastrolith), [feathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feather), impressions of skin, [internal organs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organ_\(anatomy\)) and other [soft tissues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_tissue).[^cite_note-softtissue-90][^cite_note-Schweitzer2005-94] Many fields of study contribute to our understanding of dinosaurs, including [physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics) (especially [biomechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomechanics)), [chemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemistry), [biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biology), and the [Earth sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_science) (of which [paleontology](23084.md) is a sub-discipline).[^cite_note-alexander2006-134][^cite_note-dinobiology-135] Two topics of particular interest and study have been dinosaur size and behavior.[^cite_note-136]

### Size

Main article: [Dinosaur size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_size)



Scale diagram comparing the average human to the longest known dinosaurs in five major [clades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clade):

&#x20; [Sauropoda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauropoda) (*[Supersaurus vivianae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supersaurus)*)

&#x20; [Ornithopoda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ornithopod) (*[Shantungosaurus giganteus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shantungosaurus)*)

&#x20; [Theropoda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theropoda) (*[Spinosaurus aegyptiacus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinosaurus)*)

&#x20; [Thyreophora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thyreophora) (*[Stegosaurus ungulatus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stegosaurus)*)

&#x20; [Marginocephalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marginocephalia) (*[Triceratops prorsus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triceratops)*)

Current evidence suggests that dinosaur average size varied through the Triassic, Early Jurassic, Late Jurassic and Cretaceous.[^cite_note-Sereno1999-111] Predatory theropod dinosaurs, which occupied most terrestrial carnivore niches during the Mesozoic, most often fall into the 100-to-1,000 kg (220-to-2,200 lb) category when sorted by estimated weight into categories based on [order of magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_magnitude), whereas [recent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocene) predatory carnivoran mammals peak in the 10-to-100 kg (22-to-220 lb) category.[^cite_note-JF93-137] The [mode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mode_\(statistics\)) of Mesozoic dinosaur body masses is between 1 and 10 metric tons (1.1 and 11.0 short tons).[^cite_note-Peczkis1994-138] This contrasts sharply with the average size of Cenozoic mammals, estimated by the [National Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Natural_History) as about 2 to 5 kg (4.4 to 11.0 lb).[^cite_note-NMNH-139]

The sauropods were the largest and heaviest dinosaurs. For much of the dinosaur era, the smallest sauropods were larger than anything else in their habitat, and the largest was an order of magnitude more massive than anything else that has since walked the Earth. Giant prehistoric mammals such as *[Paraceratherium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paraceratherium)* (the largest land mammal ever) were dwarfed by the giant sauropods, and only modern whales approach or surpass them in size.[^cite_note-sander2011-140] There are several proposed advantages for the large size of sauropods, including protection from predation, reduction of energy use, and longevity, but it may be that the most important advantage was dietary. Large animals are more efficient at digestion than small animals, because food spends more time in their digestive systems. This also permits them to subsist on food with lower nutritive value than smaller animals. Sauropod remains are mostly found in rock formations interpreted as dry or seasonally dry, and the ability to eat large quantities of low-nutrient browse would have been advantageous in such environments.[^cite_note-KC06-141]

#### Largest and smallest

Scientists will probably never be certain of the [largest and smallest dinosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Largest_organisms) to have ever existed. This is because only a tiny percentage of animals were ever fossilized and most of these remain buried in the earth. Few non-avian dinosaur specimens that are recovered are complete skeletons, and impressions of skin and other soft tissues are rare. Rebuilding a complete skeleton by comparing the size and morphology of bones to those of similar, better-known species is an inexact art, and reconstructing the muscles and other organs of the living animal is, at best, a process of educated guesswork.[^cite_note-142]



Comparative size of *[Argentinosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentinosaurus)* to the average human

The tallest and heaviest dinosaur known from good skeletons is *[Giraffatitan brancai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giraffatitan)* (previously classified as a species of *[Brachiosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brachiosaurus)*). Its remains were discovered in Tanzania between 1907 and 1912. Bones from several similar-sized individuals were incorporated into the skeleton now mounted and on display at the [Museum für Naturkunde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_History_Museum,_Berlin) in [Berlin](3354.md);[^cite_note-EC68-143] this mount is 12 meters (39 ft) tall and 21.8 to 22.5 meters (72 to 74 ft) long,[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145] and would have belonged to an animal that weighed between 30000 and 60000 kilograms (70000 and 130000 lb). The longest complete dinosaur is the 27 meters (89 ft) long *Diplodocus*, which was discovered in [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming) in the [United States](3434750.md) and displayed in [Pittsburgh](25101.md)'s [Carnegie Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Museum_of_Natural_History) in 1907.[^cite_note-lucas04-146] The longest dinosaur known from good fossil material is *[Patagotitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patagotitan)*: the skeleton mount in the American Museum of Natural History in [New York](645042.md) is 37 meters (121 ft) long. The [Museo Municipal Carmen Funes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museo_Carmen_Funes) in [Plaza Huincul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_Huincul), Argentina, has an *[Argentinosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentinosaurus)* reconstructed skeleton mount that is 39.7 meters (130 ft) long.[^cite_note-PLOS_One-147]



*[Bruhathkayosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruhathkayosaurus)*, potentially the largest terrestrial animal to ever exist

There were larger dinosaurs, but knowledge of them is based entirely on a small number of fragmentary fossils. Most of the largest herbivorous specimens on record were discovered in the 1970s or later, and include the massive *Argentinosaurus*, which may have weighed 80000 to 100000 kilograms (88 to 110 short tons) and reached lengths of 30 to 40 meters (98 to 131 ft); some of the longest were the 33.5-meter (110 ft) long *Diplodocus hallorum*[^cite_note-KC06-141] (formerly *Seismosaurus*), the 33-to-34-meter (108 to 112 ft) long *[Supersaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supersaurus)*,[^cite_note-LHW07-148] and 37-meter (121 ft) long *Patagotitan*; and the tallest, the 18-meter (59 ft) tall *[Sauroposeidon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauroposeidon)*, which could have reached a sixth-floor window. There were a few dinosaurs that were considered either the heaviest or longest. The most famous include *[Amphicoelias fragillimus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphicoelias_fragillimus)*, known only from a now lost partial vertebral [neural arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebra#Structure) described in 1878. Extrapolating from the illustration of this bone, the animal may have been 58 meters (190 ft) long and weighed 122400 kg (269800 lb).[^cite_note-KC06-141] However, recent research has reclassified *Amphicoelias* from the long, gracile diplodocid to the shorter but much stockier rebbachisaurid. Now renamed as *[Maraapunisaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maraapunisaurus)*, this sauropod measured as much as 40 meters (130 ft) long and weighed as much as 120000 kg (260000 lb).[^cite_note-carpenter2018-149][^cite_note-150] Another contender of this title includes *[Bruhathkayosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruhathkayosaurus)*, a controversial taxon that was recently confirmed to exist after archived photos were uncovered.[^cite_note-151] *Bruhathkayosaurus* was a titanosaur and would have most likely weighed more than even *Maraapunisaurus*. Recent size estimates in 2023 have this sauropod reaching lengths of up to 44 m (144 ft) long and placed in a colossal weight range of around 110000–170000 kg (240000–370000 lb). If these upper estimates are true, *Bruhathkayosaurus* would have rivaled the [blue whale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_whale) and *[Perucetus colossus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perucetus_colossus)* as one of the largest animals to have ever existed.[^cite_note-Bruhathkayosaurus2023-152]

The largest carnivorous dinosaur was *[Spinosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinosaurus)*, reaching a length of 12.6 to 18 meters (41 to 59 ft) and weighing 7 to 20.9 metric tons (7.7 to 23.0 short tons).[^cite_note-SMBM06-153][^cite_note-TH07-154] Other large carnivorous theropods included *[Giganotosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giganotosaurus)*, *[Carcharodontosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carcharodontosaurus)*, and *Tyrannosaurus*.[^cite_note-TH07-154] *[Therizinosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Therizinosaurus)* and *[Deinocheirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deinocheirus)* were among the tallest of the theropods. The largest ornithischian dinosaur was probably the hadrosaurid *[Shantungosaurus giganteus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shantungosaurus)*, which measured 16.6 meters (54 ft).[^cite_note-155] The largest individuals may have weighed as much as 16 metric tons (18 short tons).[^cite_note-156]



An adult [bee hummingbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bee_hummingbird), the smallest known dinosaur

The smallest dinosaur known is the [bee hummingbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bee_hummingbird),[^cite_note-157] with a length of only 5 centimeters (2.0 in) and mass of around 1.8 g (0.063 oz).[^cite_note-158] The smallest known non-[avialan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avialae) dinosaurs were about the size of [pigeons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pigeon) and were those theropods most closely related to birds.[^cite_note-zhang2008-159] For example, *[Anchiornis huxleyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchiornis_huxleyi)* is currently the smallest non-avialan dinosaur described from an adult specimen, with an estimated weight of 110 g (3.9 oz)[^cite_note-anchiadvance-160] and a total skeletal length of 34 centimeters (1.12 ft).[^cite_note-zhang2008-159][^cite_note-anchiadvance-160] The smallest herbivorous non-avialan dinosaurs included *[Microceratus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microceratus)* and *[Wannanosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wannanosaurus)*, at about 60 centimeters (2.0 ft) long each.[^cite_note-Holtz2007-161][^cite_note-butler\&zhao2009-162]

### Behavior



A nesting ground of the hadrosaur *[Maiasaura peeblesorum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maiasaura)* was discovered in 1978

Many modern birds are highly social, often found living in flocks. There is general agreement that some behaviors that are common in birds, as well as in [crocodilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocodilian) (closest living relatives of birds), were also common among extinct dinosaur groups. Interpretations of behavior in fossil species are generally based on the pose of skeletons and their [habitat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habitat_\(ecology\)), [computer simulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_simulation) of their biomechanics, and comparisons with modern animals in similar ecological niches.[^cite_note-alexander2006-134]

The first potential evidence for [herding](969684.md) or [flocking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flocking_\(behavior\)) as a widespread behavior common to many dinosaur groups in addition to birds was the 1878 discovery of 31 *Iguanodon*, ornithischians that were then thought to have perished together in [Bernissart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernissart), [Belgium](3343.md), after they fell into a deep, flooded [sinkhole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinkhole) and drowned.[^cite_note-Yans-163] Other mass-death sites have been discovered subsequently. Those, along with multiple trackways, suggest that gregarious behavior was common in many early dinosaur species. Trackways of hundreds or even thousands of herbivores indicate that duck-billed (hadrosaurids) may have moved in great herds, like the [American bison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_bison) or the African [springbok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springbok). Sauropod tracks document that these animals traveled in groups composed of several different species, at least in [Oxfordshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxfordshire), England,[^cite_note-Day2002-164] although there is no evidence for specific herd structures.[^cite_note-JLW05-165] Congregating into herds may have evolved for defense, for [migratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird_migration) purposes, or to provide protection for young. There is evidence that many types of slow-growing dinosaurs, including various theropods, sauropods, ankylosaurians, ornithopods, and ceratopsians, formed aggregations of immature individuals. One example is a site in [Inner Mongolia](55064.md) that has yielded remains of over 20 *[Sinornithomimus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinornithomimus)*, from one to seven years old. This assemblage is interpreted as a social group that was trapped in mud.[^cite_note-DVetal08sino-166] The interpretation of dinosaurs as gregarious has also extended to depicting carnivorous theropods as [pack hunters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pack_hunter) working together to bring down large prey.[^cite_note-LG93-167][^cite_note-maxwell\&ostrom1995-168] However, this lifestyle is uncommon among modern birds, crocodiles, and other reptiles (though it is not unknown as frequently assumed, and is also uncommon in mammals), and the [taphonomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taphonomy) evidence suggesting cooperative hunting in such theropods as *Deinonychus* and *[Allosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allosaurus)* can also be interpreted as the results of fatal disputes between feeding animals.[^cite_note-RB07-169]



Restoration of two *[Centrosaurus apertus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centrosaurus)* engaged in [intra-specific combat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intra-specific_combat)

The crests and frills of some dinosaurs, like the [marginocephalians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marginocephalia), theropods and [lambeosaurines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambeosaurinae), may have been too fragile to be used for active defense, and so they were likely used for sexual or aggressive displays, though little is known about dinosaur mating and [territorialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_\(animal\)). Head wounds from bites suggest that theropods, at least, engaged in active aggressive confrontations.[^cite_note-PC98-170]



Mount of a *[Gorgosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorgosaurus)*, a carnivorous theropod, attacking a herbivorous *[Zuul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zuul)*

From a behavioral standpoint, one of the most valuable dinosaur fossils was discovered in the [Gobi Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gobi_Desert) in 1971. It included a *[Velociraptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velociraptor)* attacking a *[Protoceratops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protoceratops)*,[^cite_note-AMNH-171] providing evidence that dinosaurs did indeed attack each other.[^cite_note-carpenter1998-172] Additional evidence for attacking live prey is the partially healed tail of an *[Edmontosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmontosaurus)*, a hadrosaurid dinosaur; the tail is damaged in such a way that shows the animal was bitten by a tyrannosaur but survived.[^cite_note-carpenter1998-172] [Cannibalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannibalism_\(zoology\)) amongst some species of dinosaurs was confirmed by tooth marks found in [Madagascar](18964.md) in 2003, involving the theropod *[Majungasaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majungasaurus)*.[^cite_note-rogersetal2003-173]

Comparisons between the [scleral rings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scleral_ring) of dinosaurs and modern birds and reptiles have been used to infer daily activity patterns of dinosaurs. Although it has been suggested that most dinosaurs were active during the day, these comparisons have shown that small predatory dinosaurs such as dromaeosaurids, *[Juravenator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juravenator)*, and *[Megapnosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megapnosaurus)* were likely [nocturnal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nocturnal). Large and medium-sized herbivorous and omnivorous dinosaurs such as ceratopsians, sauropodomorphs, hadrosaurids, ornithomimosaurs may have been [cathemeral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathemeral), active during short intervals throughout the day, although the small ornithischian *[Agilisaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agilisaurus)* was inferred to be [diurnal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diurnality).[^cite_note-SchmitzMotani2011-174]

Based on fossil evidence from dinosaurs such as *[Oryctodromeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oryctodromeus)*, some ornithischian species seem to have led a partially [fossorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossorial) (burrowing) lifestyle.[^cite_note-VMK07-175] Many modern birds are [arboreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arboreal) (tree climbing), and this was also true of many Mesozoic birds, especially the enantiornithines.[^cite_note-176] While some early bird-like species may have already been arboreal as well (including dromaeosaurids) such as *Microraptor*[^cite_note-chatterjee2007-177]) most non-avialan dinosaurs seem to have relied on land-based locomotion. A good understanding of how dinosaurs moved on the ground is key to models of dinosaur behavior; the science of biomechanics, pioneered by [Robert McNeill Alexander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_McNeill_Alexander), has provided significant insight in this area. For example, studies of the forces exerted by muscles and gravity on dinosaurs' skeletal structure have investigated how fast dinosaurs could run,[^cite_note-alexander2006-134] whether [diplodocids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplodocid) could create [sonic booms](183824.md) via [whip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whip)-like tail snapping,[^cite_note-goriely-178] and whether sauropods could float.[^cite_note-Henderson2006-179]

### Communication



Restoration of a striking and unusual visual display in a *[Lambeosaurus magnicristatus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambeosaurus)*. The crest may also have acted as a resonating chamber for sounds.

Modern birds [communicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_communication) by visual and auditory signals, and the wide diversity of visual display structures among fossil dinosaur groups, such as horns, frills, crests, sails, and feathers, suggests that visual communication has always been important in dinosaur biology.[^cite_note-senter2008-180] Reconstruction of the plumage color of *Anchiornis* suggest the importance of color in visual communication in non-avian dinosaurs.[^cite_note-Li2010-181] A 2009 review indicated that non-avians used visual displays and possibly non-vocal sounds, such as hissing, jaw-grinding or -clapping, splashing, and wing-beating (possible in winged maniraptoran dinosaurs).[^cite_note-senter2008-180]

Fossilized [larynxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larynx) or voice boxes have been identified in two dinosaurs; the ankylosaurid *[Pinacosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinacosaurus)* and the neornithischian *[Pulaosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulaosaurus)*, the structures of which suggest that dinosaurs were capable of complex bird-like vocalizations.[^cite_note-\:1-182][^cite_note-Yang2025-183] A 2016 study concludes that some dinosaurs may have produced closed-mouth vocalizations, such as cooing, hooting, and booming. These occur in both reptiles and birds and involve inflating the esophagus or tracheal pouches. Such vocalizations evolved independently in extant archosaurs numerous times, following increases in body size.[^cite_note-Tobias2016-184] The crests of some hadrosaurids and the nasal chambers of ankylosaurids may have been [resonators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acoustic_resonance).[^cite_note-Weishampel981-185][^cite_note-Witmer-186] Birds have evolved a novel organ for vocalization, the [syrinx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrinx_\(bird_anatomy\)),[^cite_note-\:22-187] the earliest remains of which were found in a specimen of the duck-like *[Vegavis iaai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegavis)* dated 69 –66 million years ago.[^cite_note-Clarke2016-188] The syrinx may have supplemented and then replaced the larynx as a vocal organ during bird evolution.[^cite_note-\:0-189]

### Reproductive biology

See also: [Dinosaur egg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinosaur_egg)

Three bluish eggs with black speckling sit atop a layer of white mollusk shell pieces, surrounded by sandy ground and small bits of bluish stone

Nest of a [plover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plover) (*[Charadrius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charadrius)*)

All dinosaurs laid [amniotic eggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amniote). Dinosaur eggs were usually laid in a nest. Most species create somewhat elaborate nests which can be cups, domes, plates, beds scrapes, mounds, or burrows.[^cite_note-Hansell-190] Some species of modern bird have no nests; the cliff-nesting [common guillemot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_murre) lays its eggs on bare rock, and male [emperor penguins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emperor_penguin) keep eggs between their body and feet. Primitive birds and many non-avialan dinosaurs often lay eggs in communal nests, with males primarily incubating the eggs. While modern birds have only one functional [oviduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oviduct) and lay one egg at a time, more primitive birds and other dinosaurs had two oviducts, like crocodiles. Some non-avialan dinosaurs, such as *[Troodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troodon)*, exhibited iterative laying, where the adult might lay a pair of eggs every one or two days, and then ensured simultaneous hatching by delaying [brooding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broodiness#Broodiness_in_non-avian_animals) until all eggs were laid.[^cite_note-Varrichioetal.02-191]

When laying eggs, females grow a special type of bone between the hard outer bone and the [marrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bone_marrow) of their limbs. This [medullary bone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medullary_bone), which is rich in [calcium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium), is used to make eggshells. A discovery of features in a *Tyrannosaurus* skeleton provided evidence of medullary bone in extinct dinosaurs and, for the first time, allowed paleontologists to establish the sex of a fossil dinosaur specimen. Further research has found medullary bone in the carnosaur *Allosaurus* and the ornithopod *[Tenontosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenontosaurus)*. Because the line of dinosaurs that includes *Allosaurus* and *Tyrannosaurus* diverged from the line that led to *Tenontosaurus* very early in the evolution of dinosaurs, this suggests that the production of medullary tissue is a general characteristic of all dinosaurs.[^cite_note-LW08-192]



Fossil interpreted as a nesting [oviraptorid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oviraptoridae) *[Citipati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citipati)* at the [American Museum of Natural History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Museum_of_Natural_History).&#x20;

Another widespread trait among modern birds (but see below in regards to fossil groups and extant [megapodes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megapodes)) is parental care for young after hatching. [Bob Makela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Makela) and [Jack Horner's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Horner_\(paleontologist\)) 1978 discovery of a *[Maiasaura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maiasaura)* ("good mother lizard") nesting ground in Montana demonstrated that parental care continued long after birth among ornithopods.[^cite_note-HM79-193] A specimen of the [oviraptorid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oviraptoridae) *[Citipati osmolskae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citipati)* was discovered in a [chicken-like brooding position](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken#Broodiness) in 1993,[^cite_note-search.eb-194] which may indicate that they had begun using an insulating layer of feathers to keep the eggs warm.[^cite_note-195] An embryo of the basal sauropodomorph *[Massospondylus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massospondylus)* was found without teeth, indicating that some parental care was required to feed the young dinosaurs.[^cite_note-Reiszetal05-196] Trackways have also confirmed parental behavior among ornithopods from the [Isle of Skye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Skye) in northwestern [Scotland](26994.md).[^cite_note-BBCtracks-197]

However, there is ample evidence of [precociality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precociality) or [superprecociality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precociality#Superprecociality) among many dinosaur species, particularly theropods. For instance, non-[ornithuromorph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euornithes) birds have been abundantly demonstrated to have had slow growth rates, [megapode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megapode)-like egg burying behavior and the ability to fly soon after birth.[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201] Both *Tyrannosaurus* and *Troodon* had juveniles with clear superprecociality and likely occupying different ecological niches than the adults.[^cite_note-Varrichioetal.02-191] Superprecociality has been inferred for sauropods.[^cite_note-202]

Genital structures are unlikely to fossilize as they lack scales that may allow preservation via pigmentation or residual calcium phosphate salts. In 2021, the best preserved specimen of a dinosaur's [cloacal vent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloacal_vent) exterior was described for *Psittacosaurus*, demonstrating lateral swellings similar to crocodylian musk glands used in social displays by both sexes and pigmented regions which could also reflect a signalling function. However, this specimen on its own does not offer enough information to determine whether this dinosaur had sexual signalling functions; it only supports the possibility. Cloacal visual signalling can occur in either males or females in living birds, making it unlikely to be useful to determine sex for extinct dinosaurs.[^cite_note-cloacal-vent-203]

### Physiology

Main article: [Physiology of dinosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physiology_of_dinosaurs)

Because both modern crocodilians and birds have four-chambered hearts (albeit modified in crocodilians), it is likely that this is a trait shared by all archosaurs, including all dinosaurs.[^cite_note-CH04-204] While all modern birds have high metabolisms and are [endothermic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endotherm) ("warm-blooded"), a vigorous debate has been ongoing since the 1960s regarding how far back in the dinosaur lineage this trait extended. Various researchers have supported dinosaurs as being endothermic, [ectothermic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ectotherm) ("cold-blooded"), or somewhere in between.[^cite_note-pontzer2009-205] An emerging consensus among researchers is that, while different lineages of dinosaurs would have had different metabolisms, most of them had higher metabolic rates than other reptiles but lower than living birds and mammals,[^cite_note-benson2018-206] which is termed [mesothermy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesotherm) by some.[^cite_note-grady2014-207] Evidence from crocodiles and their extinct relatives suggests that such elevated metabolisms could have developed in the earliest archosaurs, which were the common ancestors of dinosaurs and crocodiles.[^cite_note-legendre2016-208][^cite_note-seymour2004-209]



This 1897 restoration of *[Brontosaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brontosaurus)* as an aquatic, tail-dragging animal, by [Charles R. Knight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_R._Knight), typified early views on dinosaur lifestyles.

After non-avian dinosaurs were discovered, paleontologists first posited that they were ectothermic. This was used to imply that the ancient dinosaurs were relatively slow, sluggish organisms, even though many modern reptiles are fast and light-footed despite relying on external sources of heat to regulate their body temperature. The idea of dinosaurs as ectothermic remained a prevalent view until [Robert T. Bakker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_T._Bakker), an early proponent of dinosaur endothermy, published an influential paper on the topic in 1968. Bakker specifically used anatomical and ecological evidence to argue that sauropods, which had hitherto been depicted as sprawling aquatic animals with their tails dragging on the ground, were endotherms that lived vigorous, terrestrial lives. In 1972, Bakker expanded on his arguments based on energy requirements and predator-prey ratios. This was one of the seminal results that led to the dinosaur renaissance.[^cite_note-bakker1968-63][^cite_note-bakker1972-64][^cite_note-taylor2010-60][^cite_note-parsons2001-210]

One of the greatest contributions to the modern understanding of dinosaur physiology has been [paleohistology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histology), the study of microscopic tissue structure in dinosaurs.[^cite_note-erickson2014-211][^cite_note-bailleul2019-212] From the 1960s forward, [Armand de Ricqlès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armand_de_Ricql%C3%A8s) suggested that the presence of fibrolamellar bone—bony tissue with an irregular, fibrous texture and filled with blood vessels—was indicative of consistently fast growth and therefore endothermy. Fibrolamellar bone was common in both dinosaurs and pterosaurs,[^cite_note-dericqles1974-213][^cite_note-dericqles1980-214] though not universally present.[^cite_note-padian2004-215][^cite_note-desouza2020-216] This has led to a significant body of work in reconstructing [growth curves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growth_curve_\(biology\)) and modeling the evolution of growth rates across various dinosaur lineages,[^cite_note-physiologyrefs-217] which has suggested overall that dinosaurs grew faster than living reptiles.[^cite_note-bailleul2019-212] Other lines of evidence suggesting endothermy include the presence of feathers and other types of body coverings in many lineages (see [§ Feathers](#Feathers)); more consistent ratios of the isotope [oxygen-18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen-18) in bony tissue compared to ectotherms, particularly as latitude and thus air temperature varied, which suggests stable internal temperatures[^cite_note-amiot2006-218][^cite_note-amiot2010-219] (although these ratios can be altered during fossilization[^cite_note-kolodny1996-220]); and the discovery of [polar dinosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Polar_region_of_the_Cretaceous#Dinosaurs), which lived in Australia, Antarctica, and Alaska when these places would have had cool, temperate climates.[^cite_note-paul1988-221][^cite_note-clemens1993-222][^cite_note-rich2002-223][^cite_note-buffetaut2004-224]



Comparison between the [air sacs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_sac) of an [abelisaur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abelisaur) and a bird

In saurischian dinosaurs, higher metabolisms were supported by the evolution of the avian respiratory system, characterized by an extensive system of [air sacs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_sac) that extended the lungs and invaded many of the bones in the skeleton, making them hollow.[^cite_note-Sereno2008-225] Such respiratory systems, which may have appeared in the earliest saurischians,[^cite_note-oconnor2009-226] would have provided them with more oxygen compared to a mammal of similar size, while also having a larger resting [tidal volume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_volume) and requiring a lower breathing frequency, which would have allowed them to sustain higher activity levels.[^cite_note-sander2011-140] The rapid airflow would also have been an effective cooling mechanism, which in conjunction with a lower metabolic rate[^cite_note-eagle2011-227] would have prevented large sauropods from overheating. These traits may have enabled sauropods to grow quickly to gigantic sizes.[^cite_note-wedel2003-228][^cite_note-perry2009-229] Sauropods may also have benefitted from their size—their small surface area to volume ratio meant that they would have been able to thermoregulate more easily, a phenomenon termed [gigantothermy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gigantothermy).[^cite_note-sander2011-140][^cite_note-alexander1998-230]

Like other reptiles, dinosaurs are primarily [uricotelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uricotelic), that is, their [kidneys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kidney) extract nitrogenous wastes from their bloodstream and excrete it as [uric acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uric_acid) instead of [urea](31734.md) or [ammonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonia) via the ureters into the intestine. This would have helped them to conserve water.[^cite_note-benson2018-206] In most living species, uric acid is excreted along with feces as a semisolid waste.[^cite_note-tsahar2005-231][^cite_note-skadhauge2003-232] However, at least some modern birds (such as [hummingbirds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hummingbird)) can be facultatively [ammonotelic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonotelic), excreting most of the nitrogenous wastes as ammonia.[^cite_note-preest1997-233] This material, as well as the output of the intestines, emerges from the [cloaca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloaca).[^cite_note-mora1965-234][^cite_note-packard1966-235] In addition, many species regurgitate [pellets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pellet_\(ornithology\)),[^cite_note-balgooyen1971-236] and fossil pellets are known as early as the Jurassic from *[Anchiornis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchiornis)*.[^cite_note-xu2018-237]

The size and shape of the brain can be partly reconstructed based on the surrounding bones. In 1896, Marsh calculated ratios between brain weight and body weight of seven species of dinosaurs, showing that the brain of dinosaurs was proportionally smaller than in today's crocodiles, and that the brain of *Stegosaurus* was smaller than in any living land vertebrate. This contributed to the widespread public notion of dinosaurs as being sluggish and extraordinarily stupid. Harry Jerison, in 1973, showed that proportionally smaller brains are expected at larger body sizes, and that brain size in dinosaurs was not smaller than expected when compared to living reptiles.[^cite_note-russell1997-238] Later research showed that relative brain size progressively increased during the evolution of theropods, with the highest intelligence – comparable to that of modern birds – calculated for the troodontid *Troodon*.[^cite_note-brusatte2012-239]

## Origin of birds

Main article: [Origin of birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_birds)

The possibility that dinosaurs were the ancestors of birds was first suggested in 1868 by [Thomas Henry Huxley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Henry_Huxley).[^cite_note-huxley1868-240] After the work of [Gerhard Heilmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerhard_Heilmann) in the early 20th century, the [theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_theory) of birds as dinosaur descendants was abandoned in favor of the idea of them being descendants of generalized [thecodonts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thecodontia), with the key piece of evidence being the supposed lack of [clavicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clavicle) in dinosaurs.[^cite_note-heilmann-241] However, as later discoveries showed, clavicles (or a single fused [wishbone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Furcula), which derived from separate clavicles) were not actually absent;[^cite_note-KP04-19] they had been found as early as 1924 in *[Oviraptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oviraptor)*, but misidentified as an [interclavicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interclavicle).[^cite_note-HO24-242] In the 1970s, John Ostrom revived the dinosaur–bird theory,[^cite_note-ostrom1973-243] which gained momentum in the coming decades with the advent of cladistic analysis,[^cite_note-gauthier1986-244] and a great increase in the discovery of small theropods and early birds.[^cite_note-TRHJ00-35] Of particular note have been the fossils of the Jehol Biota, where a variety of theropods and early birds have been found, often with feathers of some type.[^cite_note-KP04-19][^cite_note-NYT-20161208-70] Birds share over a hundred distinct anatomical features with other theropod dinosaurs.[^cite_note-Mayretal2005-245] They are most closely allied with maniraptoran coelurosaurs.[^cite_note-KP04-19] A minority of scientists, most notably [Alan Feduccia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Feduccia) and [Larry Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Martin), have proposed other evolutionary paths, including revised versions of Heilmann's basal archosaur proposal,[^cite_note-martin2004-246] or that maniraptoran theropods are the ancestors of birds but themselves are not dinosaurs, only [convergent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convergent_evolution) with dinosaurs.[^cite_note-AF02-247]

### Feathers

Main article: [Feathered dinosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feathered_dinosaurs)



Various feathered non-avian dinosaurs, including *[Archaeopteryx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeopteryx)*, *[Anchiornis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchiornis)*, *[Microraptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microraptor)* and *[Zhenyuanlong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhenyuanlong)*

Feathers are one of the most recognizable characteristics of modern birds, and a trait that was also shared by several non-avian dinosaurs. Based on the current distribution of fossil evidence, it appears that feathers were an ancestral dinosaurian trait, though one that may have been selectively lost in some species.[^cite_note-switeknature-248] Direct fossil evidence of feathers or feather-like structures has been discovered in a diverse array of species in many non-avian dinosaur groups,[^cite_note-NYT-20161208-70] both among saurischians and ornithischians. Simple, branched, feather-like structures are known from [heterodontosaurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterodontosauridae), primitive [neornithischians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neornithischia),[^cite_note-Godefroit2014-249] and theropods,[^cite_note-Xuetal2004-250] and primitive ceratopsians. Evidence for true, vaned feathers similar to the flight feathers of modern birds has been found only in the theropod subgroup Maniraptora, which includes oviraptorosaurs, troodontids, dromaeosaurids, and birds.[^cite_note-KP04-19][^cite_note-GC06-251] Feather-like structures known as [pycnofibres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pterosaur#Pycnofibers) have also been found in pterosaurs.[^cite_note-kellneretal2009-252]

However, researchers do not agree on whether these structures share a common origin between lineages (i.e., they are [homologous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homology_\(biology\))),[^cite_note-mayr2016-253][^cite_note-benton2019-254] or if they were the result of widespread experimentation with skin coverings among ornithodirans.[^cite_note-barrett2015-255] If the former is the case, filaments may have been common in the ornithodiran lineage and evolved before the appearance of dinosaurs themselves.[^cite_note-switeknature-248] Research into the genetics of [American alligators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_alligator) has revealed that crocodylian [scutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scute) do possess feather-keratins during embryonic development, but these keratins are not expressed by the animals before hatching.[^cite_note-AlibL2006-256] The description of feathered dinosaurs has not been without controversy in general; perhaps the most vocal critics have been Alan Feduccia and Theagarten Lingham-Soliar, who have proposed that some purported feather-like fossils are the result of the decomposition of collagenous fiber that underlaid the dinosaurs' skin,[^cite_note-TLS03-257][^cite_note-FLH05-258][^cite_note-LSFX07-259] and that maniraptoran dinosaurs with vaned feathers were not actually dinosaurs, but convergent with dinosaurs.[^cite_note-AF02-247][^cite_note-FLH05-258] However, their views have for the most part not been accepted by other researchers, to the point that the scientific nature of Feduccia's proposals has been questioned.[^cite_note-Prum2003-260]

*[Archaeopteryx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeopteryx)* was the first fossil found that revealed a potential connection between dinosaurs and birds. It is considered a [transitional fossil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transitional_fossil), in that it displays features of both groups. Brought to light just two years after [Charles Darwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Darwin)'s seminal *[On the Origin of Species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Origin_of_Species)* (1859), its discovery spurred the nascent debate between proponents of [evolutionary biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_biology) and [creationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creationism). This early bird is so dinosaur-like that, without a clear impression of feathers in the surrounding rock, at least one specimen was mistaken for the small theropod *[Compsognathus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compsognathus)*.[^cite_note-PW88-261] Since the 1990s, a number of additional feathered dinosaurs have been found, providing even stronger evidence of the close relationship between dinosaurs and modern birds. Many of these specimens were unearthed in the [lagerstätten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagerst%C3%A4tten) of the Jehol Biota.[^cite_note-benton2019-254] If feather-like structures were indeed widely present among non-avian dinosaurs, the lack of abundant fossil evidence for them may be due to the fact that delicate features like skin and feathers are seldom preserved by fossilization and thus often absent from the fossil record.[^cite_note-Schweitzeretal1999-262]

### Skeleton

Because feathers are often associated with birds, feathered dinosaurs are often touted as the missing link between birds and dinosaurs. However, the multiple skeletal features also shared by the two groups represent another important line of evidence for paleontologists. Areas of the skeleton with important similarities include the neck, pubis, [wrist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrist) (semi-lunate [carpal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpal)), arm and [pectoral girdle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shoulder_girdle), furcula (wishbone), and [breast bone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keel_\(bird_anatomy\)). Comparison of bird and dinosaur skeletons through cladistic analysis strengthens the case for the link.[^cite_note-archaeopteryxucmp-263]

### Soft anatomy



Pneumatopores on the left [ilium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilium_\(bone\)) of *[Aerosteon riocoloradensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerosteon)*

Large meat-eating dinosaurs had a complex system of air sacs similar to those found in modern birds, according to a 2005 investigation led by Patrick M. O'Connor. The lungs of theropod dinosaurs (carnivores that walked on two legs and had bird-like feet) likely pumped air into hollow sacs in their skeletons, as is the case in birds. "What was once formally considered unique to birds was present in some form in the ancestors of birds", O'Connor said.[^cite_note-OConnorClaessens2005-264][^cite_note-265] In 2008, scientists described *[Aerosteon riocoloradensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerosteon)*, the skeleton of which supplies the strongest evidence to date of a dinosaur with a bird-like breathing system. [CT scanning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CT_scan) of *Aerosteon'*&#x73; fossil bones revealed evidence for the existence of air sacs within the animal's body cavity.[^cite_note-Sereno2008-225][^cite_note-newswise2-266]

### Behavioral evidence

Fossils of the troodonts *[Mei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mei_long)* and *[Sinornithoides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinornithoides)* demonstrate that some dinosaurs slept with their heads tucked under their arms.[^cite_note-XUNorell2004-267] This behavior, which may have helped to keep the head warm, is also characteristic of modern birds. Several [deinonychosaur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deinonychosauria) and oviraptorosaur specimens have also been found preserved on top of their nests, likely brooding in a bird-like manner.[^cite_note-norell1995-268] The ratio between egg volume and body mass of adults among these dinosaurs suggest that the eggs were primarily brooded by the male and that the young were highly precocial, similar to many modern ground-dwelling birds.[^cite_note-Varricchioetal2008-269]

Some dinosaurs are known to have used [gizzard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gizzard) stones like modern birds. These stones are swallowed by animals to aid digestion and break down food and hard fibers once they enter the stomach. When found in association with fossils, gizzard stones are called gastroliths.[^cite_note-wings2007-270]

## Extinction of major groups

Main article: [Cretaceous–Paleogene extinction event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretaceous%E2%80%93Paleogene_extinction_event)

All non-avian dinosaurs and most lineages of birds[^cite_note-longrich2011-271] became extinct in a [mass extinction event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extinction_event), called the [Cretaceous–Paleogene (K-Pg) extinction event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretaceous%E2%80%93Paleogene_extinction_event), at the end of the Cretaceous period. Above the [Cretaceous–Paleogene boundary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretaceous%E2%80%93Paleogene_boundary), which has been dated to 66.038 ± 0.025 million years ago,[^cite_note-renne2013-272] fossils of non-avian dinosaurs disappear abruptly; the absence of dinosaur fossils was historically used to assign rocks to the ensuing Cenozoic. The nature of the event that caused this mass extinction has been extensively studied since the 1970s, leading to the development of two mechanisms that are thought to have played major roles: an extraterrestrial [impact event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_event) in the [Yucatán Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yucat%C3%A1n_Peninsula), along with [flood basalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flood_basalt) volcanism in [India](14533.md). However, the specific mechanisms of the extinction event and the extent of its effects on dinosaurs are still areas of ongoing research.[^cite_note-brusatte2014-273] Alongside dinosaurs, many other groups of animals became extinct: pterosaurs, marine reptiles such as mosasaurs and plesiosaurs, several groups of mammals, [ammonites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonoidea) ([nautilus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nautilus)-like [mollusks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mollusca)), [rudists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudist) ([reef](240844.md)-building [bivalves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bivalvia)), and various groups of marine plankton.[^cite_note-macleod1997-31][^cite_note-archibald1982-274] In all, approximately 47% of genera and 76% of species on Earth became extinct during the K-Pg extinction event.[^cite_note-jablonski1991-275] The relatively large size of most dinosaurs and the low diversity of small-bodied dinosaur species at the end of the Cretaceous may have contributed to their extinction;[^cite_note-276] the extinction of the bird lineages that did not survive may also have been caused by a dependence on forest habitats or a lack of adaptations to [eating seeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seed_predation) for survival.[^cite_note-field2018-277][^cite_note-larson2016-278]

### Pre-extinction diversity

Just before the K-Pg extinction event, the number of non-avian dinosaur species that existed globally has been estimated at between 628 and 1078.[^cite_note-leloeuff2012-279] It remains uncertain whether the diversity of dinosaurs was in gradual decline before the K-Pg extinction event, or whether dinosaurs were actually thriving prior to the extinction. Rock formations from the [Maastrichtian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maastrichtian) epoch, which directly preceded the extinction, have been found to have lower diversity than the preceding [Campanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campanian) epoch, which led to the prevailing view of a long-term decline in diversity.[^cite_note-macleod1997-31][^cite_note-archibald1982-274][^cite_note-carpenter1983-280] However, these comparisons did not account either for varying [preservation potential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preservation_potential) between rock units or for different extents of exploration and excavation.[^cite_note-brusatte2014-273] In 1984, [Dale Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale_Russell) carried out an analysis to account for these biases, and found no evidence of a decline;[^cite_note-russell1984-281] another analysis by David Fastovsky and colleagues in 2004 even showed that dinosaur diversity continually increased until the extinction,[^cite_note-fastovsky2004-282] but this analysis has been rebutted.[^cite_note-sullivan2006-283] Since then, different approaches based on statistics and mathematical models have variously supported either a sudden extinction[^cite_note-brusatte2014-273][^cite_note-leloeuff2012-279][^cite_note-chiarenza2019-284] or a gradual decline.[^cite_note-lloyd2011-285][^cite_note-sakamoto2016-286] End-Cretaceous trends in diversity may have varied between dinosaur lineages: it has been suggested that sauropods were not in decline, while ornithischians and theropods were in decline.[^cite_note-barrett2009-287][^cite_note-upchurch2011-288]

### Impact event

Main article: [Chicxulub crater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicxulub_crater)



[Luis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Walter_Alvarez) (left) and his son [Walter Alvarez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Alvarez) (right) at the K-T Boundary in [Gubbio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gubbio), Italy, 1981



The [Chicxulub Crater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicxulub_Crater) at the tip of the [Yucatán Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yucat%C3%A1n_Peninsula); the impactor that formed this crater may have caused the dinosaur [extinction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extinction).

The [bolide impact hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alvarez_hypothesis), first brought to wide attention in 1980 by [Walter Alvarez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Alvarez), [Luis Alvarez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Walter_Alvarez), and colleagues, attributes the K-Pg extinction event to a [bolide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolide) (extraterrestrial projectile) impact.[^cite_note-289] Alvarez and colleagues proposed that a sudden increase in [iridium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iridium) levels, recorded around the world in rock deposits at the Cretaceous–Paleogene boundary, was direct evidence of the impact.[^cite_note-alvarez1980-290] [Shocked quartz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shocked_quartz), indicative of a strong shockwave emanating from an impact, was also found worldwide.[^cite_note-bohor1987-291] The actual impact site remained elusive until a [crater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicxulub_crater) measuring 180 km (110 mi) wide was discovered in the Yucatán Peninsula of southeastern [Mexico](3966054.md), and was publicized in a 1991 paper by [Alan Hildebrand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_R._Hildebrand) and colleagues.[^cite_note-hildebrand1991-292] Now, the bulk of the evidence suggests that a bolide 5 to 15 kilometers (3 to 9+1⁄2 miles) wide impacted the Yucatán Peninsula 66 million years ago, forming this crater[^cite_note-pope1996-293] and creating a "kill mechanism" that triggered the extinction event.[^cite_note-schulte2010-294][^cite_note-kring2007-295][^cite_note-chiarenza2020-296]

Within hours, the Chicxulub impact would have created immediate effects such as earthquakes,[^cite_note-ivanov2005-297] tsunamis,[^cite_note-matsui2002-298] and a global firestorm that likely killed unsheltered animals and started wildfires.[^cite_note-robertson2004-299][^cite_note-robertson2013-300] However, it would also have had longer-term consequences for the environment. Within days, sulfate [aerosols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerosol) released from rocks at the impact site would have contributed to [acid rain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid_rain) and [ocean acidification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocean_acidification).[^cite_note-pope1997-301][^cite_note-ohno2014-302] [Soot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soot) aerosols are thought to have spread around the world over the ensuing months and years; they would have cooled the surface of the Earth by reflecting [thermal radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermal_radiation), and greatly slowed [photosynthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photosynthesis) by blocking out sunlight, thus creating an [impact winter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_winter).[^cite_note-brusatte2014-273][^cite_note-kaiho2016-303][^cite_note-lyons2020-304] (This role was ascribed to [sulfate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfate) aerosols until experiments demonstrated otherwise.[^cite_note-ohno2014-302]) The cessation of photosynthesis would have led to the collapse of [food webs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_web) depending on leafy plants, which included all dinosaurs save for grain-eating birds.[^cite_note-larson2016-278]

### Deccan Traps

Main article: [Deccan Traps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_Traps)

At the time of the K-Pg extinction, the [Deccan Traps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_Traps) flood basalts of India were actively erupting. The eruptions can be separated into three phases around the K-Pg boundary, two prior to the boundary and one after. The second phase, which occurred very close to the boundary, would have extruded 70 to 80% of the volume of these eruptions in intermittent pulses that occurred around 100,000 years apart.[^cite_note-chenet2009-305][^cite_note-schoene2019-306] [Greenhouse gases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gases) such as [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide) and [sulfur dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfur_dioxide) would have been released by this volcanic activity,[^cite_note-mclean1985-307][^cite_note-self2006-308] resulting in [climate change](5042951.md) through temperature perturbations of roughly 3 °C (5.4 °F) but possibly as high as 7 °C (13 °F).[^cite_note-tobin2017-309] Like the Chicxulub impact, the eruptions may also have released sulfate aerosols, which would have caused acid rain and global cooling.[^cite_note-schmidt2016-310] However, due to large error margins in the dating of the eruptions, the role of the Deccan Traps in the K-Pg extinction remains unclear.[^cite_note-renne2013-272][^cite_note-brusatte2014-273][^cite_note-hofman2000-311]

Before 2000, arguments that the Deccan Traps eruptions—as opposed to the Chicxulub impact—caused the extinction were usually linked to the view that the extinction was gradual. Prior to the discovery of the Chicxulub crater, the Deccan Traps were used to explain the global iridium layer;[^cite_note-mclean1985-307][^cite_note-sahni1988-312] even after the crater's discovery, the impact was still thought to only have had a regional, not global, effect on the extinction event.[^cite_note-glasby1996-313] In response, Luis Alvarez rejected volcanic activity as an explanation for the iridium layer and the extinction as a whole.[^cite_note-alvarez1987-314] Since then, however, most researchers have adopted a more moderate position, which identifies the Chicxulub impact as the primary progenitor of the extinction while also recognizing that the Deccan Traps may also have played a role. Walter Alvarez himself has acknowledged that the Deccan Traps and other ecological factors may have contributed to the extinctions in addition to the Chicxulub impact.[^cite_note-alvarez1997-315] Some estimates have placed the start of the second phase in the Deccan Traps eruptions within 50,000 years after the Chicxulub impact.[^cite_note-renne2015-316] Combined with mathematical modelling of the [seismic waves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seismic_wave) that would have been generated by the impact, this has led to the suggestion that the Chicxulub impact may have triggered these eruptions by increasing the permeability of the [mantle plume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mantle_plume) underlying the Deccan Traps.[^cite_note-richards2015-317][^cite_note-khazins2019-318]

Whether the Deccan Traps were a major cause of the extinction, on par with the Chicxulub impact, remains uncertain. Proponents consider the climatic impact of the sulfur dioxide released to have been on par with the Chicxulub impact, and also note the role of flood basalt volcanism in other mass extinctions like the [Permian-Triassic extinction event](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permian-Triassic_extinction_event).[^cite_note-archibald2010-319][^cite_note-courtillot2010-320] They consider the Chicxulub impact to have worsened the ongoing climate change caused by the eruptions.[^cite_note-keller2014-321] Meanwhile, detractors point out the sudden nature of the extinction and that other pulses in Deccan Traps activity of comparable magnitude did not appear to have caused extinctions. They also contend that the causes of different mass extinctions should be assessed separately.[^cite_note-schulte2010reply-322] In 2020, Alfio Chiarenza and colleagues suggested that the Deccan Traps may even have had the opposite effect: they suggested that the long-term warming caused by its carbon dioxide emissions may have dampened the impact winter from the Chicxulub impact.[^cite_note-chiarenza2020-296]

### Possible Paleocene survivors

Non-avian dinosaur remains have occasionally been found above the K-Pg boundary. In 2000, [Spencer Lucas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spencer_G._Lucas) and colleagues reported the discovery of a single hadrosaur right femur in the [San Juan Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan_Basin) of [New Mexico](21649.md), and described it as evidence of Paleocene dinosaurs. The rock unit in which the bone was discovered has been dated to the early [Paleocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleocene) epoch, approximately 64.8 million years ago.[^cite_note-fassett2011-323] If the bone was not [re-deposited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deposition_\(geology\)) by weathering action, it would provide evidence that some dinosaur populations survived at least half a million years into the Cenozoic.[^cite_note-fassett2009-324] Other evidence includes the presence of dinosaur remains in the Hell Creek Formation up to 1.3 m (4.3 ft) above the Cretaceous–Paleogene boundary, representing 40,000 years of elapsed time. This has been used to support the view that the K-Pg extinction was gradual.[^cite_note-sloan1986-325] However, these supposed Paleocene dinosaurs are considered by many other researchers to be [reworked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reworked_fossil), that is, washed out of their original locations and then reburied in younger sediments.[^cite_note-lucas2009-326][^cite_note-renne2012-327][^cite_note-lofgren1990-328] The age estimates have also been considered unreliable.[^cite_note-koenig2012-329]

## Cultural depictions

Main article: [Cultural depictions of dinosaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_depictions_of_dinosaurs)



Outdated *[Iguanodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iguanodon)* statues created by [Benjamin Waterhouse Hawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Waterhouse_Hawkins) for the [Crystal Palace Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystal_Palace_Park) in 1853

*[Gertie the Dinosaur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gertie_the_Dinosaur)* (1914) by [Winsor McCay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winsor_McCay), featuring the first animated dinosaur

By human standards, dinosaurs were creatures of fantastic appearance and often enormous size. As such, they have captured the popular imagination and become an enduring part of human culture. The entry of the word "dinosaur" into the common [vernacular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vernacular) reflects the animals' cultural importance: in English, "dinosaur" is commonly used to describe anything that is impractically large, obsolete, or bound for extinction.[^cite_note-m-w-330]

Public enthusiasm for dinosaurs first developed in [Victorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_era) England, where in 1854, three decades after the first scientific descriptions of dinosaur remains, a menagerie of lifelike [dinosaur sculptures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystal_Palace_dinosaurs) was unveiled in [London](17867.md)'s [Crystal Palace Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystal_Palace_Park). The Crystal Palace dinosaurs proved so popular that a strong market in smaller replicas soon developed. In subsequent decades, dinosaur exhibits opened at parks and [museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_history_museum) around the world, ensuring that successive generations would be introduced to the animals in an immersive and exciting way.[^cite_note-torrens1993-331] The enduring popularity of dinosaurs, in its turn, has resulted in significant public funding for dinosaur science, and has frequently spurred new discoveries. In the United States, for example, the competition between museums for public attention led directly to the Bone Wars of the 1880s and 1890s, during which a pair of feuding paleontologists made enormous scientific contributions.[^cite_note-breithaupt1997-332]

The popular preoccupation with dinosaurs has ensured their appearance in [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature), [film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film), and other [media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_\(communication\)). Beginning in 1852 with a passing mention in [Charles Dickens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Dickens)' *[Bleak House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bleak_House)*,[^cite_note-bleakhouse-333] dinosaurs have been featured in large numbers of [fictional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiction) works. [Jules Verne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jules_Verne)'s 1864 novel *[Journey to the Center of the Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journey_to_the_Center_of_the_Earth)*, [Sir Arthur Conan Doyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Conan_Doyle)'s 1912 book *[The Lost World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lost_World_\(Doyle_novel\))*, the 1914 animated film *[Gertie the Dinosaur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gertie_the_Dinosaur)* (featuring the first animated dinosaur), the iconic 1933 [film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_picture) *[King Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Kong_\(1933_film\))*, the 1954 *[Godzilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Godzilla_\(1954_film\))* and its many sequels, the best-selling 1990 novel *[Jurassic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic_Park_\(novel\))* by [Michael Crichton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Crichton) and its 1993 [film adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic_Park_\(film\)) are just a few notable examples of dinosaur appearances in fiction. Authors of general-interest [non-fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-fiction) works about dinosaurs, including some prominent paleontologists, have often sought to use the animals as a way to educate readers about science in general. Dinosaurs are ubiquitous in [advertising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advertising); numerous [companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_\(law\)) have referenced dinosaurs in printed or televised advertisements, either in order to sell their own products or in order to characterize their rivals as slow-moving, dim-witted, or obsolete.[^cite_note-DFGlut1997-334][^cite_note-335]

[^cite_note-cold-blooded-7]: &#x20;Dinosaurs (including birds) are members of the [natural group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clade) [Reptilia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reptilia). Their biology does not precisely correspond to the antiquated [class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_\(biology\)) Reptilia of [Linnaean taxonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linnaean_taxonomy), consisting of [cold-blooded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ectotherm) [amniotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amniote) without fur or feathers. As Linnaean taxonomy was formulated for modern animals prior to the study of evolution and paleontology, it fails to account for extinct animals with intermediate traits between traditional classes.
